class om.cam.core.GenerateHMReportOptions(enum.IntFlag)
Flags to modify the behaviour of JobListSet.GenerateHMReport.
Enumeration value Description
OVERRIDE_FOLDER Override already existing folder. If not given and the
folder exists the function will fail.
STOP_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS Stop report generation if any of the given jobs is not up-
to-date.
CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS Continue report generation even if jobs are not up-to-
date.
ASK_CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS Asks the user whether to continue report generation in
case outdated jobs are encountered.
OUTDATED_JOB_STRATEGY_MASK Mask for the job strategy bits
STOP_ON_INVALID_JOBS Stop report generation if any of the given jobs is invalid.
CONTINUE_ON_INVALID_JOBS Continue report generation even if any of the given jobs
is invalid.
ASK_CONTINUE_ON_INVALID_JOBS Asks the user whether to continue report generation in
case invalid jobs are encountered.
INVALID_JOB_STRATEGY_MASK Mask for the invalid job strategy mask
An example how to set and reset bits with enum.IntFlag can be found here:
Some enum operations.
import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
#Alias
Options = camcore.GenerateHMReportOptions
# Dump all values
for name, value in Options.__members__.items():
print(f"{name} = {value}")
# Set a bit with OR
value = Options.CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS | Options.CONTINUE_ON_INVALID_JOBS
gui.Write(f"ContinueOnOutdatedJobs|ContinueOnInvalidJobs = {str(value)}")
gui.Write(f"int(ContinueOnOutdatedJobs|ContinueOnInvalidJobs) = {int(value)}")
# Reset a bit with AND NOT

-- 307 of 389 --

value = Options.OUTDATED_JOB_STRATEGY_MASK & ~Options.CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS
gui.Write(f"OutdatedJobStrategyMask & ~ContinueOnOutdatedJobs = {str(value)}")
gui.Write(f"int(OutdatedJobStrategyMask & ~ContinueOnOutdatedJobs) = {int(value)}")

Configuration parameters and properties
class om.cam.core.CfgParameters
This is a helper class to manage the configuration parameters of a hyperMILL object.
The optional mode parameter in the methods determines, in which context the config parameters are
valid. See ConfigIO for more details. By default it is set to ConfigIO.WRITE_EDIT. And behaves like a
user input.
Methods
GetAll(mode: ConfigIO = ConfigIO.WRITE_EDIT) → dict[str, str]
Get all configuration parameters in a dictionary of key, value pairs.
Parameters: mode (ConfigIO) – Context mode of the request.

-- 312 of 389 --

Get the value of a single configuration parameter using its key.
Parameters: key (str) – Key (name) of the requested configuration parameter.
mode (ConfigIO) – Context mode of the request.
SetAll(cfg: dict, mode: ConfigIO = ConfigIO.WRITE_EDIT) → bool
Set all configuration parameters that are given in the dictionary. Existing values will be overwritten,
non-existing will be ignored.
Parameters: str (dict) – Key/value string dictionary with values to be overwritten.
mode (ConfigIO) – Context mode of the request.
Set(key: str, value: str, mode: ConfigIO = ConfigIO.WRITE_EDIT) → bool
Set a single configuration parameter. If the set is successfull the return will be True else it returns
False.
Parameters: key (str) – Key (name) of the configuration parameter to be set.
value (str) – New value of the parameter.
mode (ConfigIO) – Context mode of the request.
Count(mode: ConfigIO = ConfigIO.WRITE_EDIT) → int
Get the number of configuration entries of the object (a single entry is a pair of [key: str , value:
str]).

class om.cam.core.Property_(CamObj)
A property assigns a value to a unique name, like an key/value pair in a dictionary. At the moment, only
string values are supported. It’s commonly used in a property set PropertySet_.

class om.cam.core.PropertySet_(CamObj)
A property set is a collection of properties of type Property_. It’s used in many classes.
Example for using property sets and properties
import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
job_lists: list[camcore.JobList] = camcore.GetCamEntities(camcore.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBL
for joblist in job_lists:
gui.Write(f"Joblist '{joblist.Name}'")
# for prop in joblist.GetPropertySet().ToList(): # returns an unsorted list
for key, prop in joblist.PropertySet.GetProperties().items(): # sorted and searchable
value = prop.Value if prop.Value else "None (or not printable)"
gui.Write(f" '{key}' = '{value}'")
Magic methods
__len__() → int
Returns the length of the object.
__str__() → str
Returns a string representation of the object.
__repr__() → str
Returns a more information-rich, string representation of the object.
__getitem__(name: str | int) → Property_
Returns the property at the given key or index or None.
Properties
property UUID(get) → str
Get the UUID of the object.
Methods
ToDict() → dict[str, str]
This method is deprecated. Use GetProperties() instead.
Copies the property set to a Python dictionary. The dictionary cannot be used to update the
property set!
ToList() → list[Property_]
Get a list of all properties.
GetProperties() → dict[str, Property_]
Get a dictionary that contains all properties of the property set.
class om.cam.core.UserVariables(CamObj)
This class manages the user variables of a hyperMILL document.
An example is given here:

-- 314 of 389 --

Example how to use the user variables.
import om
if __name__ == "__main__":
app = om.GetApplication()
userVariables = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel.UserVariables
userVariables.SetVariable("MyVariable", 42.00)
for key in userVariables.GetKeys():
value = userVariables[key]
print(f"{key} = {value}", file=app)

-- 319 of 389 --

A more sophisticated example with UI creating jobs, translating and moving around
# Collect all jobs of same type into a compound job.
import om
import om.cam.core as cam
import om.cam.gui as gui
import sys
if __name__ == '__main__':
app = om.GetApplication()
jlset = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel.JobListSet
name = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel.Name
job_lists: list[cam.JobList] = cam.GetCamEntities(cam.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS)
if len(job_lists) == 0:
gui.ShowMessageBox("No joblists found.")
sys.exit(0)
# use the last joblist
job_list = job_lists[-1]
# Let's show a parameter dialog
cycle_desc = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.STRING,
"Cycle Description")
cycle_desc.Value = "Ccc"
dlg = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Please enter a cycle description",
cycle_desc)
dlgResult = dlg.Show()
if dlgResult != gui.DialogResult.ACCEPTED:
sys.exit(1)
cycleDescValue = cycle_desc.Value.lower()
# Let's create a new compound job in the joblist.
compoundJob = job_list.CreateCompoundJob()
compoundJobName = None
# list comprehension of joblist.GetJobs()
jobsToMove = [it for it in job_list.GetJobs() if it.JobType.lower() == cycleDescValue]
if len(jobsToMove) == 0:
gui.ShowMessageBox("No jobs to move.")
else:
for job in jobsToMove:
jlset.Move(job.UUID, compoundJob.UUID)
if not compoundJobName:
compoundJobName = app.Translate(job.CycleName)
if len(compoundJob.GetJobs()) == 0:
# Remove the compoundjob
jlset.Remove(compoundJob.UUID)
else:
# Rename the compound job
jlset.Rename(compoundJob.UUID, compoundJobName)
Remove(uuid: str)
Remove an element from the tree of the set.
Parameters: uuid (str) – UUID of the element to be removed.
Example A more sophisticated example with UI creating jobs, translating and moving around
RemoveStockChain(uuids: list[str]) → bool
Remove the stock chain for the given job browser elements (job list, compound job, job).

Iterates over all jobs and checks each tool that is used in the job
import om.cam.core as core
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
job_lists: list[core.JobList] = core.GetCamEntities(core.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS)
for job_list in job_lists:
cnt_tool_changes = 0
last_tool_id = None
for job in job_list.GetJobs():
tool = job.GetTool()
if tool and last_tool_id != tool.ID:
cnt_tool_changes += 1
gui.Write(f"Tool change at job '{job.Name}' to {tool.Name} ({tool.ID})")
last_tool_id = tool.ID
gui.ShowMessageBox(f"Joblist '{job_list.Name}' has {cnt_tool_changes} tool changes

GetFrame() → Frame
Get the frame that is used in the job.
Show and edit features for all jobs
import om.cam.core as core
import om.cam.gui as gui
def PrintFrame(job: core.Job):
frame = job.GetFrame()
if frame:
gui.WriteMessage(f"Job={job.Name} -> Frame {frame.Name} (UUID={frame.UUID})")
else:

-- 327 of 389 --

gui.WriteMessage(f"Job={job.Name} has no frame")
if __name__ == '__main__':
jobs = core.GetCamEntities(core.CamEntityFilter.ALL_CYCLE_JOBS)
for job in jobs:
saved_frame = job.GetFrame()
if saved_frame is None:
continue
gui.WriteMessage("Original frame")
PrintFrame(job)
gui.WriteMessage("Frame removed")
job.SetFrame(None)
PrintFrame(job)
gui.WriteMessage("Frame restored")
# Restore with UUID
# if saved_frame is not None:
# job.SetFrame(saved_frame.GetUUID())
# or restore directly
job.SetFrame(saved_frame)
PrintFrame(job)

import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
def print_features(job: camcore.Job):
idx = 0
features = job.GetFeatures()
for feat in features:
idx = idx+1
gui.Write(f"Job={job.Name} -> Feature ({idx}) {feat.Name}")

-- 328 of 389 --

if __name__ == '__main__':
jobs: list[camcore.Job] = camcore.GetCamEntities(camcore.CamEntityFilter.ALL_CYCLE_JOB
for job in jobs:
saved_feats = job.GetFeatures()
if len(saved_feats) == 0:
continue
gui.Write("Original features")
print_features(job)
gui.Write("Features removed")
job.SetFeatures(None)
print_features(job)
gui.Write("Features restored")
job.SetFeatures(saved_feats)
print_features(job)

Event handling
class om.cam.core.EventDispatcher
The class EventDispatcher is used to dispatch events from native C++ to Python. It’s intended to be
used as a property in an object instance.
Magic methods
__len__() → int
Returns the length of the object.
__str__() → str
Returns a string representation of the object.
__repr__() → str
Returns a more information-rich, string representation of the object.
Methods
Add(func: Callable[[dict], None], ident: str = '') → int
Register a new function to be dispatched.
Parameters: func (Callable[[dict], None]) – Function to be registered. The function must be of
type def myFunc(params: dict) -> None.
ident (str) – Optional identifier string for the method. Can be used to remove the
event. If ident is not unique, all elements with this identifier will be removed.
Remove(keyOrIdent: int | str) → int
Remove a function with the given “key” from the event.
Parameters: keyOrIdent (Union[int, str]) – Key from add() to the function to be removed.
Clear() → None
Remove all registered functions from the dispatcher.

How to include
To include om.cam.gui in your Python code, place these lines in your code.
import om.cam.core
import om.cam.gui
om.cam.gui always needs the core module om.cam.core too!

class om.cam.gui.FrameBrowserCommand(enum.IntEnum)
Command id used in hyperMILL Frame Browser

-- 358 of 389 --

Enumeration value Description
PRESELECT Preselect a frame/origin
REMOVE_PRESELECTION Remove the preselection
NEW_FRAME Create a new frame
NEW_FRAME_FROM_ORIGIN Create a new frame from the selected origin
NEW_ORIGIN Create a new origin
NEW_ORIGIN_FROM_FRAME Create a new origin from the selected frame
EDIT Edit the selected item
DELETE Delete the selected item
COPY Copy the selected item to clipboard
CUT Cut the selected item to clipboard

ParameterDescription
class om.cam.gui.ParameterDescription(CamObj)
A ParameterDescription contains the information necessary to query or display a parameter in a UI, like
its name, type, default value, tool tip and so on. To change a single parameter using a
ParameterDescription use a ParameterDialog or the CreateParameterDialog function with a parameter
description as argument.

Parameters: type (ParameterType) – The type of the parameter.
name (str) – The identify (property name) used internally.
defaultValue (object) – Default value of the parameter. For

pre-selected value.

A ParameterGroup contains a list of parameters that are used together under a common name, e.g. the
“Red”, “Green” and “Blue” components of a color.

CreateParameterDialog function with a ParameterGroup as argument.

Basic example

-- 372 of 389 --

Demonstrates how to create your first program
Basic example how to include om.cam.core
import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
# Our main routine with guard
if __name__ == '__main__':
jobs = camcore.GetCamEntities(camcore.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBS)
if len(jobs) > 0:
text = "These are the job lists:\n"
for job in jobs:
text += f"[{job.ID}] {job.Name}\n"
else:
text = "No jobs found."
gui.ShowMessageBox(text, gui.Severity.MESSAGE)
Download basic_example.py
Application example
Demonstrates how to get the CAM application from the CAD/CAM application.
Demonstrates how to get the CAM application.
import om
import om.cad.core
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
# The cadcam app manages the CAD and the CAM part of the document
app = om.GetApplication()
cad_model = app.CurrentDocument.CadModel
# We want to access the CAM methods in the cam model
cam_model = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel
# Properties
app_prop_set = cam_model.AppPropertySet.GetProperties()
# Name of the CAM model
cam_name = cam_model.Name
# Get the system information
sys_info = app.SystemInfo
# hyperMILL Version
version = sys_info.CamVersion
# Current language
lang = sys_info.CamLanguage
# Path to the global working space
gws = sys_info.CamGlobalWorkingSpace
# Path to the projects directory
proj_dir = sys_info.CamProjectsDirectory
# Get the full executable path for the application
app_name = "Tool Database"
tdb_path = sys_info.GetCamUtilAppPath(app_name)
# Get the full executable path for the DLL
dll_name = "om90CoBsx64.dll"
dll_path = sys_info.GetCamFilePath(dll_name)
# Get the localization for the ID
localization_id = "MissingMirrorPlane"
message = sys_info.GetCamMessage("hmMdMain.loc", localization_id)

-- 373 of 389 --

msg = f"CAM model: {cam_name}\n"
msg += f"Version: {version}\nLanguage: {lang}\n"
msg += f"Global working space: {gws}\nProjects directory: {proj_dir}\n"
msg += f"Executable for \"{app_name}\": {tdb_path}\n"
msg += f"File path for \"{dll_name}\": {dll_path}\n"
msg += f"Localization for \"{localization_id}\": {message}\n"
msg += f"CAM App properties: {app_prop_set}\n"
gui.ShowMessageBox(msg, gui.Severity.MESSAGE,
gui.MessageBoxButtons.BUTTON_OK)
Download app_example.py
Using GetCamEntities
Demonstrates how to use GetCamEntities with filters.
Using om.cam.GetCamEntities with filters
from om.cam.core import *
import om.cam.gui as gui
# If you don't like lambdas, a normal function will do the same.
def hasName(obj: object):
return hasattr(obj, "Name"),
if __name__ == '__main__':
# Print all joblists
joblists = GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS)
for joblist in joblists:
gui.Write(f"JLID={joblist.ID}, JLName={joblist.Name}")
# Returns the first three jobs and compound jobs in the first joblist.
# Compound jobs dont have an ID, so don't print it.
jobs1 = filter(
lambda job: job.JobList.ID == 1 and (hasattr(job, "ID") and job.ID <= 3),
GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBS)
)
for job in jobs1:
gui.Write(f"[{job.ID}] Name=\"{job.Name}\", JobType=\"{job.JobType}\", UUID=\"{job.UUID}\"
# Get all joblists starting with RGP
joblistNames = []
for joblist in filter(lambda jl: jl.Name.startswith("RGP"), GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL
joblistNames.append(joblist.Name)
# Show all 3D Optimized Roughing (Slr3) for joblist starting with RGP
jobs2 = filter(
lambda job: job.JobList.Name in joblistNames and job.JobType=="Slr3",
GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL_CYCLE_JOBS)
)
for job in jobs2:
gui.Write(f"JL=\"{job.JobList.Name}\": [{job.ID}] Name=\"{job.Name}\", JobType=\"{job.JobT
# Show multiple types
frames = GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL_FRAMES | CamEntityFilter.ALL_ORIGINS)
for frame in frames:
gui.Write(f"Name=\"{frame.Name}\", UUID=\"{frame.UUID}\"")
# All entities with a name and with a normal function, instead of a lambda
entities = filter(
hasName,
GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL)
)

-- 374 of 389 --

for e in entities:
gui.Write(f"Name=\"{e.Name}\", EntityType=\"{type(e)}\"")

Count tool changes
Demonstrates how to count tool changes in a joblist
Reads the tool of each job and counts the tool changes
import om.cam.core as core
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
job_lists: list[core.JobList] = core.GetCamEntities(core.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS)
for job_list in job_lists:
cnt_tool_changes = 0
last_tool_id = None
for job in job_list.GetJobs():
tool = job.GetTool()
if tool and last_tool_id != tool.ID:
cnt_tool_changes += 1
gui.Write(f"Tool change at job '{job.Name}' to {tool.Name} ({tool.ID})")
last_tool_id = tool.ID
gui.ShowMessageBox(f"Joblist '{job_list.Name}' has {cnt_tool_changes} tool changes", copyT
Download count_toolchanges.py
Count tool changes v2
Demonstrates how to count tool changes in a joblist (second version)
Counts the tool changes before and after an optimization
# Counts all tool changes in the joblist set
import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
def count_tool_changes(job_list: camcore.JobList) -> int:
cnt_tool_changes = 0
last_tool_id = None
for job in job_list.GetJobs():
tool = job.GetTool()
if tool:
if last_tool_id != tool.ID:
cnt_tool_changes += 1
last_tool_id = tool.ID
return cnt_tool_changes
if __name__ == '__main__':
job_lists: list[camcore.JobList] = camcore.GetCamEntities(camcore.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS
for job_list in job_lists:
before = count_tool_changes(job_list)
job_list.OptimizeWithSorting(camcore.JobListOptimization.OPTIMIZE,
camcore.JobListOptWithToolSorting.ASC_DIAM,
camcore.JobListOptWithCompoundJobs.OFF)
after = count_tool_changes(job_list)

A more sophisticated example with UI creating jobs, translating and moving around
# Collect all jobs of same type into a compound job.
import om
import om.cam.core as cam
import om.cam.gui as gui
import sys
if __name__ == '__main__':
app = om.GetApplication()
jlset = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel.JobListSet
name = app.CurrentDocument.CamModel.Name
job_lists: list[cam.JobList] = cam.GetCamEntities(cam.CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBLISTS)
if len(job_lists) == 0:
gui.ShowMessageBox("No joblists found.")
sys.exit(0)
# use the last joblist
job_list = job_lists[-1]
# Let's show a parameter dialog
cycle_desc = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.STRING,
"Cycle Description")
cycle_desc.Value = "Ccc"
dlg = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Please enter a cycle description",
cycle_desc)
dlgResult = dlg.Show()
if dlgResult != gui.DialogResult.ACCEPTED:
sys.exit(1)
cycleDescValue = cycle_desc.Value.lower()
# Let's create a new compound job in the joblist.
compoundJob = job_list.CreateCompoundJob()
compoundJobName = None
# list comprehension of joblist.GetJobs()
jobsToMove = [it for it in job_list.GetJobs() if it.JobType.lower() == cycleDescValue]
if len(jobsToMove) == 0:
gui.ShowMessageBox("No jobs to move.")
else:
for job in jobsToMove:
jlset.Move(job.UUID, compoundJob.UUID)
if not compoundJobName:
compoundJobName = app.Translate(job.CycleName)
if len(compoundJob.GetJobs()) == 0:
# Remove the compoundjob
jlset.Remove(compoundJob.UUID)

-- 376 of 389 --

else:
# Rename the compound job
jlset.Rename(compoundJob.UUID, compoundJobName)

Enum operations
Some enum operations
Some enum operations
import om.cam.core as camcore
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
#Alias
Options = camcore.GenerateHMReportOptions
# Dump all values
for name, value in Options.__members__.items():
print(f"{name} = {value}")
# Set a bit with OR
value = Options.CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS | Options.CONTINUE_ON_INVALID_JOBS
gui.Write(f"ContinueOnOutdatedJobs|ContinueOnInvalidJobs = {str(value)}")
gui.Write(f"int(ContinueOnOutdatedJobs|ContinueOnInvalidJobs) = {int(value)}")
# Reset a bit with AND NOT
value = Options.OUTDATED_JOB_STRATEGY_MASK & ~Options.CONTINUE_ON_OUTDATED_JOBS
gui.Write(f"OutdatedJobStrategyMask & ~ContinueOnOutdatedJobs = {str(value)}")
gui.Write(f"int(OutdatedJobStrategyMask & ~ContinueOnOutdatedJobs) = {int(value)}")

import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
# A single parameter of type "String" with the name "Project Name" is defined
projectNameParameter = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.STRING, "Project Name")
# The current value for each parameter can be set using the "SetValue" function.
projectNameParameter.Value = "This could be your project name!"
# A dialog is created to query for the single parameter.
# The title of the dialog will be "Project Description"
dialog = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Project Description", projectNameParameter)
# The dialog is now shown as modal dialog, the script will not continue until the user either
# Show() will return PyDialogResult::Accepted, PyDialogResult::Rejected or PyDialogResult::Err
dialogResult = dialog.Show()
if dialogResult == gui.DialogResult.ACCEPTED:
# If the user accepted (pressed "OK") and no error occured, the parameter description for
# can be queried using the name of the parameter:
# Now you can access the actual value provided by the user:
print(f"Project name = {projectNameParameter.Value}")

Parameter Group
import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
# Defining three parameters, representing a 3-D coordinate
pX = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "X", 1.2)
pY = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Y", 3.4)
pZ = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Z", 5.6)
# The three coordinates are now combined into one group,
# the title of the group will be "Origin".
pgOrigin = gui.ParameterGroup("Origin", [pX, pY, pZ])
# A dialog is created to query the parameters in the group.
# The title of the dialog will be "Set Origin"
dialog = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Set Origin", pgOrigin)
# The dialog is now shown as modal dialog, the script will not continue until the user either
# Show() will return PyDialogResult::Accepted, PyDialogResult::Rejected or PyDialogResult::Err
dialogResult = dialog.Show()
if(dialogResult == gui.DialogResult.ACCEPTED):
# If the user accepted (pressed "OK") and no error occured, the new values for origin can
print(f"{pX.Name}={pX.Value}")
print(f"{pY.Name}={pY.Value}")
print(f"{pZ.Name}={pZ.Value}")

import om.cam.gui as gui
if __name__ == '__main__':
# This example shows how the user can create several parameter groups and combine them into a
# The group list is then used to create a dialog to query the values for the parameters.
# In this example two 3D coordinates should be queried: a start point and an end point
# First defining the coordinates for start and end point
pStartX = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "X", -1.2)
pStartY = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Y",-3.4)
pStartZ = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Z", -5.6)
pEndX = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "X", 1.2)
pEndY = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Y", 3.4)
pEndZ = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.FLOAT64, "Z", 5.6)
# Now creating groups for the start and and point
pStart = gui.ParameterGroup("Start", [pStartX, pStartY, pStartZ])
pEnd = gui.ParameterGroup("End", [pEndX, pEndY, pEndZ])
# Now creating the group list to be used
pGroupList = gui.ParameterGroupList([pStart, pEnd])
# A dialog is created to query the parameters in both groups.
# The title of the dialog will be "Set Translation"
dialog = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Set Translation", pGroupList)
# The dialog is now shown as modal dialog, the script will not continue until the user either
# Show() will return PyDialogResult::Accepted, PyDialogResult::Rejected or PyDialogResult::Err
dialogResult = dialog.Show()
if dialogResult == gui.DialogResult.ACCEPTED:
# If the user accepted (pressed "OK") and no error occured, the new values for origin can
# We can access each parameter description in a parameter group using the "Descriptions" f
print(pStart.Name)
for description in pStart.GetDescriptions():
print(f"{description.Name}: {description.Value}")
print(pEnd.Name)

String list parameter dialog
The example shows how to open a string list dialog.
Example of a string list as parameter.
from om.cam.core import (
CamEntityFilter,
GetCamEntities,
)
from om.cam.gui import (
CreateParameterDialog,
DialogResult,
ParameterDescription,
ParameterType,
Severity,
ShowMessageBox,
)
# Main
if __name__ == '__main__':
jobs = GetCamEntities(CamEntityFilter.ALL_JOBS)
jobNames = [job.Name for job in jobs] # Get only the job names
# Pre-select a job
selected = jobNames[-1] # pre-select the last job by name
# selected = 2 # pre-select the third job by index, -1 is the last index
# (jobNames, selected) are a tuple
param = ParameterDescription(ParameterType.STRING_LIST, "Jobs", (jobNames, selected), "My jobs
dlg = CreateParameterDialog("Select a job", param)
result = dlg.Show()
if result == DialogResult.ACCEPTED:
ShowMessageBox(f"You have selected job '{param.Value}'")
else:
ShowMessageBox("You haven't selected any job.", severity=Severity.WARNING)

import om.cam.gui as gui
# If you want to define your own enums in Python, use the "enum" module provided by the Python Sta
import enum
if __name__ == '__main__':
# Custom Python Enums
# Here we define our own enum (simply some different kinds of animals) purely in Python, no co
# For more details how to define enums in Python, please consult the documentation for the "en
Animal = enum.Enum('Animal', [('CAT', 3), ('DOG', 5), ('DUCK', 7), ('ELEPHANT', 13), ('PIG', 1

parameterOne = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.ENUM, "Some Animals", currentAnimal)
# Predefined Enums
# If you want to use an enum that is already exposed in the Python API, simply create a varia
# and then use it to create a PyParameterDescription with type "enum".
buttonValue = gui.MessageBoxButtons.BUTTON_YES
parameterTwo = gui.ParameterDescription(gui.ParameterType.ENUM, "Button Value", buttonValue)
# To allow the user to select both parameters, put them into a PyParameterDescriptionList
parameterGroup = gui.ParameterGroup("Enums", [parameterOne, parameterTwo])
# A dialog is created to query for the parameters create for the enums.
# The title of the dialog will be "Using Enums"
dialog = gui.CreateParameterDialog("Using Enums", parameterGroup)

# Show() will return PyDialogResult::Accepted, PyDialogResult::Rejected or PyDialogResult::Err

hmObjGroup)

# create a new joblist

-- 386 of 389 --

ma = paraMa.Value
if ma is not None:
joblist.SetModel(ma)
# assign the selected stock to joblist
st = paraStock.Value
if st is not None:
joblist.SetStock(st)
# create a profile finishing job
job = joblist.CreateJob("Prof")
# assign the selected tool to job
tool = paraTool.Value

import om.cam.gui as gui

# Display message, warnings and error message boxes.
gui.SetMessageMode(gui.MessageModes.INFOS)
gui.Write("Here I am!", severity=gui.Severity.MESSAGE)
gui.ShowMessageBox("Here I am!")
# Message boxes in the indented block will be ignored.
with gui.DisableHyperMillMessages():
gui.ShowMessageBox("Now I'm hidden.", copyToOutput=True)
gui.ShowMessageBox("Hello again!", copyToOutput=True)

import om.cam.core
import inspect
module_objects = inspect.getmembers(om.cam._hmCoreX64)
defined_classes = [obj for name, obj in module_objects if
inspect.isclass(obj) and inspect.getmodule(obj) == om.cam._hmCoreX64]
for cls in defined_classes:

OPEN MIND Coding Style
We recommend writing application scripts using PEP 8 coding style.
“How to Write Beautiful Python Code With PEP 8” is a good article to introduce PEP8. It begins:
“PEP 8 exists to improve the readability of Python code. But why is readability so important? Why is writing
readable code one of the guiding principles of the Python language, according to the Zen of Python?

-- 387 of 389 --

As Guido van Rossum said, “Code is read much more often than it is written.” You may spend a few
minutes, or a whole day, writing a piece of code to process user authentication. Once you’ve written it,
you’re never going to write it again. But you’ll definitely have to read it again. That piece of code might
remain part of a project you’re working on. Every time you go back to that file, you’ll have to remember what
that code does and why you wrote it, so readability matters.
If you’re new to Python, it can be difficult to remember what a piece of code does a few days, or weeks,
after you wrote it. If you follow PEP 8, you can be sure that you’ve named your variables well. You’ll know
that you’ve added enough whitespace so it’s easier to follow logical steps in your code. You’ll also have
commented your code well. All this will mean your code is more readable and easier to come back to. As a
beginner, following the rules of PEP 8 can make learning Python a much more pleasant task.
Following PEP 8 is particularly important if you’re looking for a development job. Writing clear, readable
code shows professionalism. It’ll tell an employer that you understand how to structure your code well.
If you have more experience writing Python code, then you may need to collaborate with others. Writing
readable code here is crucial. Other people, who may have never met you or seen your coding style before,
will have to read and understand your code. Having guidelines that you follow and recognize will make it
easier for others to read your code.”
References
PEP 8 - Style Guide for Python Code
How to Write Beautiful Python Code With PEP 8
Zen of Python
An inspiration for you to write beautiful scripts:
Beautiful is better than ugly.
Explicit is better than implicit.
Simple is better than complex.
Complex is better than complicated.
Flat is better than nested.
Sparse is better than dense.
Readability counts.
Special cases aren’t special enough to break the rules.
Although practicality beats purity.
Errors should never pass silently.
Unless explicitly silenced.
In the face of ambiguity, refuse the temptation to guess.
There should be one– and preferably only one –obvious way to do it.
Although that way may not be obvious at first unless you’re Dutch.

-- 388 of 389 --

Now is better than never.
Although never is often better than right now.
If the implementation is hard to explain, it’s a bad idea.
If the implementation is easy to explain, it may be a good idea.
Namespaces are one honking great idea – let’s do more of those!
